<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem: perturbed dynamics}
    Let $P = P^* \in \mathcal{A}$. There exists a unique group of automorphisms $\tau^P$ generated by the derivation $\mathcal{L} + \mathcal{L}_P$. For every $A \in \mathcal{A}$, $\tau^P$ can be obtained by the absolutely converging series
    \begin{align*}
        \tau_t^P  (A) = \sum_{n = 0}^\infty \mathrm{i}^n \int_0^t \mathrm{d} t_1 \int_0^{t_1} \mathrm{d} t_2 \dots \int_0^{t_{n-1}} \mathrm{d} t_n \, [\tau_{t_n} (P), \,  [\dots [\tau_{t_1} (P), \, \tau_t (A)]]] \, .
    \end{align*}
Duhamel's formula gives, for all $A\in\mathcal{A}$,
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert\tau_t^P(A)-\tau_t(A)\rVert
        &\leq 2|t|\,\lVert P\rVert\,\lVert A\rVert\, .
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
Let $(\mathcal{A}, \tau)$ be a $C^*$-dynamical system and $\beta \in \mathbb{R}$. We denote by $\mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$ the set of $\tau$-entire analytic elements of $\mathcal{A}$. We call a state a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state if it satisfies 
    \begin{align*}
        \omega(A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i}  \beta} (B)) = \omega( B A)
    \end{align*}
for all $A \in \mathcal{A}$ and $B \in \mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$. For $\beta = 0$ we additionally require $\omega\circ\tau_t = \omega$ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}$\footnote{For $\beta \neq 0$ this property follows automatically \cite[Proposition 5.3.3]{BR2}.}.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
The set $\mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$ is dense in $\mathcal{A}$. Moreover, if $P = P^* \in \mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$ then for any $A \in \mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$ it also holds that $A \in \mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^{\tau^P}$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:dec funct}
    We call a function $F : [0, \infty) \to (0, \infty)$ a decay function if $F$ is non-increasing, $F(0) = 1$ and $\log (F)$ is convex.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:norm}
    Let $F$ be a decay function, and $\mathbb{E}$ the fermionic conditional expectation introduced in Proposition \ref{Ex+UniqueExpectation}. We say that an observable $A \in \mathcal{A}_\Gamma$ is $F$-localized if for all $x \in \Gamma$ 
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert A \rVert_{F, x} \coloneq \lVert A \rVert + \sup_{k \in \mathbb{N}_0} \frac{\lVert A - \mathbb{E}_{B_k (x)} A \rVert}{F(k)} < \infty \, .
    \end{align*}
    We denote the set of all $F$-localized observables by $\mathcal{A}_F$. We set $\mathcal{A}_F^+ \coloneq \mathcal{A}_F \cap \mathcal{A}_\Gamma^+$. For $\nu > 0$ and $F( \cdot) = (1 + \cdot)^{- \nu}$ we abbreviate $\lVert \cdot \rVert_{\nu, x} \coloneq \lVert \cdot \rVert_{F, x}$ and $\mathcal{A}_\nu\coloneq\mathcal{A}_F$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{norm interaction}
    For an interaction $\varPhi$ and some decay function $F$, we define
    \begin{align*}
        \|\varPhi\|_{F} \coloneq \sup_{x\in \Gamma} \sum_{\substack{Z\in
        P_0(\Gamma)\\x\in Z}} \frac{\| \varPhi(Z) \|}{F(\mathrm{diam}(Z))}  ~,
    \end{align*}
    where $\mathrm{diam}(Z) = \max \{ d(x, y) ~|~ x, \, y \in Z \}$. The set of interactions with finite $\norm{\cdot}_{F}$ is denoted by $\mathcal{P}_{F}$.
    For $\nu \geq 0$ and $F( \cdot ) = (1 + \cdot)^{-\nu}$ we abbreviate $\norm{\cdot}_\nu \coloneq \norm{\cdot}_F$ and $\mathcal{P}_\nu \coloneq \mathcal{P}_F$.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def: quasi-local terms}
    Fix once and for all a strict total order $<$ on $\Gamma$. For each $M \in P_0 (\Gamma)$, we define the center of $M$ as $\mathrm{C}(M)\in M$, where $\mathrm{C}(M)$ minimizes the map $M\to\mathbb{R}_+$, $x\mapsto\sum_{y\in M}d(x,y)$. If there are several such points, choose the smallest one with respect to $<$. Let $x\in \Gamma$. We define $R_x \subseteq P_0(\Gamma)$ to be the set of all finite subsets of $\Gamma$ that have their center in $x$. Given an interaction $\varPhi$ we define 
    \begin{align*}
        \varPhi_x \coloneq \sum_{M \in R_x} \varPhi(M) \, .
    \end{align*}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem: bound zero chain}
    Note that for any decay function $F : \mathbb{R}_+ \to \mathbb{R}_+$, interaction $\varPhi \in \mathcal{P}_F$ and lattice point $x$ the quasi-local observable $\varPhi_x$ is well-defined and that $\lVert \varPhi_x \rVert_{F,x} \leq 3 \, \lVert \varPhi \rVert_F $.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
If $\Gamma$ is embedded in $\mathbb{R}^D$, then the strict total order can be defined by the lexicographical order. For an explicit construction, see \cite{BTW26}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
We say that the dynamics $\tau$ satisfies a Lieb--Robinson bound with $\zeta_\mathrm{LR} ~:~ P_0 (\Gamma) \times P_0(\Gamma) \times [0, \infty) \to [0, \infty)$ if
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert [\tau_t (A), \, B] \rVert \leq \lVert A \rVert \, \lVert B \rVert \, \zeta_\mathrm{LR} (X, Y, |t|) \, ,
    \end{align*}
    for all $X$, $Y \in P_0(\Gamma)$, $A \in \mathcal{A}_X^+$, $B \in  \mathcal{A}_Y$ and $t \in \mathbb{R}$.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm: QBP}
    Let $(\mathcal{A}, \tau)$ be a $C^*$-dynamical system, $\omega$ a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state, and $P = P^* \in \mathcal{A}$. Then there exists a norm convergent Dyson series $\eta_P \in \mathcal{A}$, explicitly given by Equation \eqref{eq: eta}, such that
    \begin{align*}
        \omega_P(A) = \omega(\eta_P \, A \, \eta_P^*) \qquad \forall A \in \mathcal{A} \, .
    \end{align*}
    In particular, $ A \mapsto \eta_P A \eta_P^*$ is a completely positive bounded map transporting $\omega$ to $\omega_P$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
Formally, there is a similar concept for ground states of gapped Hamiltonians, see, for example, \cite{H07b, BMNS11, MO19, BTW26}. In this setting, one constructs an automorphism that maps the ground state of a gapped Hamiltonian to the ground state of another gapped Hamiltonian, assuming that the spectral gap does not close along a path of Hamiltonians connecting them. There are some important differences to the map constructed in the previous theorem. The map between KMS states constructed here is completely positive, but need not preserve the unit. In the lattice setting, locality of the implementing operator near the perturbation does not imply that its conjugation map acts as the identity on distant observables. Indeed, even if $[\eta_P,A]=0$, we have
    \[
        \eta_P A\eta_P^*-A=A(\eta_P\eta_P^*-1).
    \]
    The right-hand side need not be small. Thus the locality of the implementing operator must be distinguished from locality of the observable map near the support of $A$. In particular, it need not approach the identity on observables far from the perturbation. Constructing transport maps with stronger locality and product properties remains a separate problem.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop: QBP differential equation}
Let $(\mathcal{A}, \tau)$ be a $C^*$-dynamical system, $\beta \in \mathbb{R}$, $\omega$ a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state, and $P = P^* \in \mathcal{A}$. Then for any $A \in \mathcal{A}$ and $s \in [0, 1]$ the following differential equation holds:
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \omega_{s P} (A) = - \frac{\beta}{2} \, \omega_{s P} \bigl( \bigl\{ \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P - \omega_{s P} (P)),  \, A \bigr\} \bigr) \, . \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation} \label{eq: QBP differential eqtn. prop}
\end{align*}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor: cont. KMS state}
    Let $(\mathcal{A}, \tau)$ be a $C^*$-dynamical system, $\omega$ a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state, and $P = P^* \in \mathcal{A}$. Then
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_P (A) - \omega (A)| \leq |\beta| \, \lVert A \rVert \, \lVert P \rVert 
    \end{align*}
    for all $A \in \mathcal{A}$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
For $\beta=0$ the two states agree. Assume $\beta\neq0$. By stationarity and normalization of $f_\beta$,
    \begin{align*}
        \omega_{sP}\bigl(\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{sP}}(P)\bigr)=\omega_{sP}(P).
    \end{align*}
    Proposition \ref{prop: QBP differential equation} and the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality for $\omega_{sP}$ give
    \begin{align*}
        \left|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s}\omega_{sP}(A)\right|
        &\leq |\beta|\,\norm A
        \sqrt{\omega_{sP}\bigl((\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{sP}}(P))^2\bigr)
                     -\omega_{sP}(P)^2}\\
        &\leq |\beta|\,\norm A\,\norm{\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{sP}}(P)}
        \leq |\beta|\,\norm A\,\norm P.
    \end{align*}
    Integrating over $s\in[0,1]$ proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def: decay of corr}
   Let $\beta \in \mathbb{R}$ and $\omega$ be a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state on $\mathcal{A}_\Gamma$. We say that $\omega$ satisfies decay of correlations with respect to the non-increasing function $\zeta_\mathrm{cor}: [0, \infty) \to (0, \infty)$, the non-decreasing function $f_\mathrm{cor}: [0, \infty) \to [0, \infty)$ and $n_\mathrm{cor} \in \mathbb{N}_0$ if
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega(A B) - \omega(A) \, \omega(B)| \leq \lVert A \rVert \, \lVert B \rVert \, |X|^{n_\mathrm{cor}} \, f_\mathrm{cor}(|Y|) \, \zeta_\mathrm{cor}(d(X, Y)) \, ,
    \end{align*}
    for all $X$, $Y \in P_0 (\Gamma)$ and all $A \in \mathcal{A}_X$, $B \in \mathcal{A}_Y$, where one of them is even. We require $\lim_{r \to \infty} \zeta_\mathrm{cor} (r) = 0$. Moreover, we assume that $f_\mathrm{cor}(r) \geq 1$ for $r \geq 1$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
For clarity in our exposition we have chosen not to symmetrize the bound in the cardinality of $X$ and $Y$. For disjoint supports the observables commute, since one is even. Interchanging $A$ and $B$ therefore gives the same estimate with $|X|^{n_\mathrm{cor}}f_\mathrm{cor}(|Y|)$ replaced by $|Y|^{n_\mathrm{cor}}f_\mathrm{cor}(|X|)$. As in the preceding work \cite{CMTW25}, we allow the dependence on the support of one of the operators to be exponential, while the dependence on the other support must be polynomial. In the long-range, high-temperature setting of \cite{MSAC26}, the clustering assumption allows exponential dependence on both supports.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\label{def: uniform doc}
    Let $\beta \in \mathbb{R}$ and $\omega$ be a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state on $\mathcal{A}_\Gamma$. Let $V \in \mathcal{P}_F$ be an extensive perturbation. We say that $\omega$ satisfies $V$-uniform decay of correlations with respect to $\zeta_\mathrm{cor}$, $f_\mathrm{cor}$ and $n_\mathrm{cor}$ if the states $\omega_{s V_M}$ satisfy decay of correlations uniformly in $M \in P_0 (\Gamma)$ and $s \in [0, 1]$.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
\label{def: LPPL}
    Let $\beta \in \mathbb{R}$ and $\omega$ be a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state on $\mathcal{A}_\Gamma$. We say that $\omega$ satisfies LPPL with respect to the non-decreasing functions $f_\mathrm{LPPL}: [0, \infty) \to [0, \infty)$, $g_\mathrm{LPPL}: [0, \infty) \to [0, \infty)$ and the non-increasing function $\zeta_\mathrm{LPPL}: [0, \infty) \to [0, \infty)$ if
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_P (A) - \omega (A)| \leq \lVert A \rVert  \, f_\mathrm{LPPL} (|Y| )  \, g_\mathrm{LPPL}(\lVert P \rVert_{F, x}) \, \zeta_\mathrm{LPPL} (d(x, Y))  \, ,
    \end{align*}
    for all $Y \in P_0(\Gamma)$, $x \in \Gamma$ and $P = P^* \in \mathcal{A}_F^+$, $A \in \mathcal{A}_Y$. We require $\lim_{r\to\infty}\zeta_\mathrm{LPPL}(r)=0$.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
\label{def: LI}
    Let $\beta \in \mathbb{R}$ and $\omega$ be a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state on $\mathcal{A}_\Gamma$. We fix an extensive perturbation $V \in \mathcal{P}_F$. We say that $\omega$ satisfies LI with respect to the non-increasing function $\zeta_\mathrm{LI}: [0, \infty) \to [0, \infty)$ and some non-decreasing function $f_\mathrm{LI}: [0, \infty) \to [0, \infty)$, $g_\mathrm{LI}: [0, \infty) \to [0, \infty)$ if
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{V_Z} (A) - \omega_{V_Y} (A)| \leq \lVert A \rVert \, f_\mathrm{LI} (|X|) \,  g_\mathrm{LI}(\lVert V \rVert_F) \, \zeta_\mathrm{LI} (d(X, Z \setminus Y))  \, ,
    \end{align*}
    for all $X \subsetneq Y \subsetneq Z \in P_0 (\Gamma)$ and $A \in \mathcal{A}_X$. We require that $ \lim_{r \to \infty}\zeta_\mathrm{LI} (r) =0$.
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
LI is sometimes described by saying that enlarging a finite box has an asymptotically negligible effect on observables far from its boundary. Our definition is stronger, since the bound is uniform over all finite regions, including disconnected ones.
\\
Consider the nearest-neighbor ferromagnetic Ising model on $\mathbb Z^2$ at zero magnetic field and inverse temperature $\beta>\beta_c$, with $H=0$ and open boundary conditions for the finite patches of $V$. Along increasing boxes, the states $\omega_{V_X}$ converge to the equal-weight mixture of the plus and minus phases by spin-flip symmetry and \cite[Propositions 6.9 and 8.5]{GHM01}. Nevertheless, LI fails. Indeed, let $r\geq1$, choose $x,y\in\mathbb Z^2$ with $d(x,y)>2r+1$, and set $X=\{x,y\}$ and $Y=B_r(x)\cup B_r(y)$. Since the two balls do not interact, factorization and spin-flip symmetry give
\begin{align*}
    \omega_{V_Y}(\sigma_x^z\sigma_y^z)=0.
\end{align*}
Writing $m_\beta=\omega^+(\sigma_0^z)>0$, convergence along boxes and Griffiths' inequality show that a sufficiently large box $Z\supsetneq Y$ can be chosen such that
\begin{align*}
    \omega_{V_Z}(\sigma_x^z\sigma_y^z)\geq\frac{m_\beta^2}{2}.
\end{align*}
Since $|X|=2$ and $d(X,Z\setminus Y)>r$, LI would imply
\begin{align*}
    0<\frac{m_\beta^2}{2}
    &\leq\bigl|\omega_{V_Z}(\sigma_x^z\sigma_y^z)
              -\omega_{V_Y}(\sigma_x^z\sigma_y^z)\bigr|\\
    &\leq f_\mathrm{LI}(2)g_\mathrm{LI}(\lVert V\rVert_F)\zeta_\mathrm{LI}(r).
\end{align*}
Sending $r\to\infty$ gives a contradiction. Thus considering only connected boxes does not capture LI.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop: convergence from li}
    Let $H,V\in\mathcal{P}_F$ for a decay function $F$, and assume that $\mathcal{L}_{H+sV}$ generates a dynamics $\tau^{sV}$ for every $s\in[0,1]$. Let $\omega$ be a $(\tau^{0V},\beta)$-KMS state. Fix $s\in[0,1]$ and assume that $\omega$ satisfies LI for $sV$. For any IAS $(\Lambda_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$, set $V_n=V_{\Lambda_n}$. Then there is a $(\tau^{sV},\beta)$-KMS state $\omega_{sV}$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\omega_{sV_n}(A)=\omega_{sV}(A),\qquad A\in\mathcal{A}_\Gamma.
    \end{align*}
    The limit is independent of the IAS. If LI holds for all $sV$, $s\in[0,1]$, with the same functions $\zeta_\mathrm{LI}$, $f_\mathrm{LI}$ and $g_\mathrm{LI}$, then the convergence is uniform in $s\in[0,1]$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Let $A\in\mathcal{A}_X$. For all sufficiently large $n$ and $m>n$, LI gives
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{sV_m} (A) - \omega_{sV_n} (A)| & \leq \norm A \,  f_\mathrm{LI}(|X|) \, g_\mathrm{LI}(s\norm V_F)
        \zeta_\mathrm{LI}(d(X,\Lambda_m\setminus\Lambda_n))\\
        &\leq \norm A f_\mathrm{LI}(|X|)g_\mathrm{LI}(\norm V_F)
        \zeta_\mathrm{LI}(d(X,\Gamma\setminus\Lambda_n)).
    \end{align*}
    Every finite ball around $X$ is eventually contained in $\Lambda_n$. Hence the last expression tends to zero, uniformly in $s$ whenever the LI functions are uniform. For fixed $s$, the limit defines a linear functional on $\mathcal{A}_0$ satisfying
    \begin{align*}
        \omega_{sV}(1)=1,\qquad
        |\omega_{sV}(A)|\leq\norm A,\qquad
        \omega_{sV}(A^*A)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\omega_{sV_n}(A^*A)\geq0.
    \end{align*}
    It therefore extends uniquely to a state on $\mathcal{A}_\Gamma$. For $A\in\mathcal{A}_\Gamma$ and $A_k\in\mathcal{A}_0$ with $\norm{A-A_k}\to0$, we calculate
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{sV}(A)-\omega_{sV_n}(A)|
        &\leq 2\norm{A-A_k}
        +|\omega_{sV}(A_k)-\omega_{sV_n}(A_k)|.
    \end{align*}
    First choosing $k$ and then $n$ proves convergence on $\mathcal{A}_\Gamma$, with the same uniformity in $s$. Lemma \ref{lem: str conv time evol} and \cite[Proposition 5.3.25]{BR2} show that the limit is a $(\tau^{sV},\beta)$-KMS state.

    To show independence of the IAS, let $(\widetilde\Lambda_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ be another IAS. For $A\in\mathcal{A}_X$, choose $n$ so large that both $\Lambda_n$ and $\widetilde\Lambda_n$ contain the fattening $X_r$ for a given $r$ of $X$. Comparing both states with the state for $\Lambda_n\cup\widetilde\Lambda_n$ gives
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{sV_{\Lambda_n}}(A)-\omega_{sV_{\widetilde\Lambda_n}}(A)|
        &\leq 2\norm A f_\mathrm{LI}(|X|)g_\mathrm{LI}(\norm V_F)\zeta_\mathrm{LI}(r).
    \end{align*}
    Taking $n\to\infty$ and then $r\to\infty$ proves equality of the limits on $\mathcal{A}_0$, and hence on $\mathcal{A}_\Gamma$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
LI does not imply uniqueness of the KMS state for the perturbed dynamics, even if the reference dynamics is trivial while $V$ generates a nontrivial dynamics. Consider the spin system on $\mathbb Z^2$ and define
    \begin{align*}
        V(\{x\})&=-4\sigma_x^z,\\
        V(\{x,y\})&=-\sigma_x^z\sigma_y^z+\sigma_x^z+\sigma_y^z,
        \qquad d(x,y)=1,
    \end{align*}
    with $V(M)=0$ otherwise. For a finite region $\Lambda$,
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{M\subseteq\Lambda}V(M)
        =-\sum_{\substack{\{x,y\}\subseteq\Lambda\\d(x,y)=1}}
             \sigma_x^z\sigma_y^z
         -\sum_{x\in\Lambda}
             \bigl(4-\deg_\Lambda(x)\bigr)\sigma_x^z,
    \end{align*}
    where $\deg_\Lambda(x)=|\{y\in\Lambda:d(x,y)=1\}|$. This is the nearest-neighbor Ising Hamiltonian with $+$ boundary condition. At sufficiently large $\beta$, these Gibbs states satisfy exponential decay of correlations uniformly over all finite $\Lambda$ by \cite[Corollary~7.11 and Theorem~7.12]{GHM01}. Arbitrary regions are covered by the volume monotonicity in \cite[Eq.~(14)]{GHM01}, since the connection event in Theorem~7.12 is decreasing. Since $V$ is finite-range, Theorems \ref{thm: LPPL without doc} and \ref{thm: li from lppl} imply LI for the tracial state with respect to $V$. The estimate extends to quantum observables because diagonal projection in the $\sigma^z$-basis preserves expectations and products on disjoint supports, and contracts the operator norm.
    \\
    In infinite volume, the four linear edge contributions at each site cancel the singleton term. Hence $V$ generates the standard zero-field Ising dynamics. The states selected by its finite patches converge to the plus phase, whereas for $\beta>\beta_c$ the same dynamics has the two distinct KMS states $\omega^+$ and $\omega^-$. Thus LI selects a state for the specified finite patches but does not imply uniqueness. It also depends on the chosen interaction, rather than only on the infinite-volume dynamics.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop: loc of phi}
    Let $\varPsi$ and $V$ be interactions, $P=P^*\in\mathcal{A}_\Gamma^+$ and $\beta\in\mathbb{R}$. For $X\in P_0(\Gamma)$, let $\tau^{X,P}$ be the dynamics generated by $\mathcal{L}_\varPsi+\mathcal{L}_{V_X+P}$. We distinguish two regimes:
    \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Polynomial decay.} Fix $C_\mathrm{int}>0$, $\nu>0$ and $\mu>3D+\nu$. Assume that $\varPsi,V\in\mathcal{P}_\mu$, $P\in\mathcal{A}_\nu^+$ and
    \begin{align*}
        \norm\varPsi_\mu+\norm V_\mu<C_\mathrm{int}.
    \end{align*}
    Then there are polynomials $f$ and $h$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        \norm{\tau_t^{X,P}(A)}_{\nu,x}
        &\leq f(|t|)(1+\norm P_{\nu,x})\norm A_{\nu,x},\\
        \norm{\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{X,P}}(A)}_{\nu,x}
        &\leq h(|\beta|)(1+\norm P_{\nu,x})\norm A_{\nu,x}
    \end{align*}
    for $t\in\mathbb{R}$, $x\in\Gamma$ and $A\in\mathcal{A}_\nu$. Their coefficients depend only on the lattice, $\mu$, $\nu$ and $C_\mathrm{int}$, and can be chosen uniformly in $X$, $P$, $\varPsi$ and $V$ subject to these assumptions.

    \item \textbf{Exponential decay.} Fix $a,b,c>0$ such that $b > 4 a$ and $c > 8 a$. There exists $C_\mathrm{int}=C_\mathrm{int}(a,c)>0$ such that, if $P\in\mathcal{A}_{\exp(-b\cdot)}^+$ and
    \begin{align*}
        \norm\varPsi_{\exp(-c\cdot)}+\norm V_{\exp(-c\cdot)}
        <\frac{C_\mathrm{int}}{1+|\beta|},
    \end{align*}
    then
    \begin{align*}
        \norm{\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{X,P}}(A)}_{\exp(-a\cdot),x}
        \leq C(1+|\beta|)(1+\norm P_{\exp(-b\cdot),x})
        \norm A_{\exp(-b\cdot),x}
    \end{align*}
    for $x\in\Gamma$ and $A\in\mathcal{A}_{\exp(-b\cdot)}$. The constant $C>0$ depends only on the lattice, $a$, $b$ and $c$, and is uniform in $X$, $P$, $\beta$, $\varPsi$ and $V$ subject to these assumptions.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm: LPPL}
    Let $H$ be an interaction, $\tau$ the dynamics generated by $\mathcal{L}_H$, $\beta\in\mathbb{R}$, and $\omega$ a $(\tau,\beta)$-KMS state. Fix $P=P^*\in\mathcal{A}_\Gamma^+$ and assume that $\omega_{sP}$ satisfies decay of correlations with respect to $\zeta_\mathrm{cor}$, $f_\mathrm{cor}$ and $n_\mathrm{cor}$, uniformly in $s\in[0,1]$.
    \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Polynomial decay.} Fix $C_\mathrm{int}>0$, $\nu>0$ and $\mu>3D+\nu$. Assume that $H\in\mathcal{P}_\mu$, $P\in\mathcal{A}_\nu^+$ and
    \begin{align*}
        \norm H_\mu<C_\mathrm{int},\qquad
        (1+r)^{-\nu}\leq(1+r)^{Dn_\mathrm{cor}}\zeta_\mathrm{cor}(r),\quad r\geq0.
    \end{align*}
    Then there is a polynomial $g$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_P(A)-\omega(A)|
        &\leq g(|\beta|)\norm A f_\mathrm{cor}(|Y|)
           (1+d(x,Y))^{Dn_\mathrm{cor}}\zeta_\mathrm{cor}(d(x,Y)/2)\\
        &\quad\times(1+\norm P_{\nu,x})\norm P_{\nu,x}.
    \end{align*}

    \item \textbf{Exponential decay.} Fix $a,b,c>0$ such that $b> 4 a$ and $c> 8 a$, and let $C_\mathrm{int}=C_\mathrm{int}(a,c)$ be as in Proposition \ref{prop: loc of phi}. Assume that $H\in\mathcal{P}_{\exp(-c\cdot)}$, $P\in\mathcal{A}_{\exp(-b\cdot)}^+$ and
    \begin{align*}
        \norm H_{\exp(-c\cdot)}&<\frac{C_\mathrm{int}}{1+|\beta|}, \qquad \exp(-ar) \leq(1+r)^{Dn_\mathrm{cor}}\zeta_\mathrm{cor}(r),\qquad r\geq0.
    \end{align*}
    Then there is a constant $C>0$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_P(A)-\omega(A)|
        &\leq C|\beta|(1+|\beta|)\norm A f_\mathrm{cor}(|Y|)
          (1+d(x,Y))^{Dn_\mathrm{cor}}\zeta_\mathrm{cor}(d(x,Y)/2)\\
        &\quad\times(1+\norm P_{\exp(-b\cdot),x})
          \norm P_{\exp(-b\cdot),x}.
    \end{align*}
    \end{enumerate}
    Both bounds hold for $x\in\Gamma$, $Y\in P_0(\Gamma)$ and $A\in\mathcal{A}_Y$. The coefficients of $g$ and the constant $C$ are independent of $H$, $P$ and $\beta$ subject to the stated restrictions.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm: LPPL without doc}
    Let $H,V$ be interactions, $X\in P_0(\Gamma)$, and $\tau^X$ the dynamics generated by $\mathcal{L}_H+\mathcal{L}_{V_X}$. Let $\beta\in\mathbb{R}$ and let $\omega_{V_X}$ be a $(\tau^X,\beta)$-KMS state satisfying decay of correlations with respect to $\zeta_\mathrm{cor}$, $f_\mathrm{cor}$ and $n_\mathrm{cor}$. Fix $P=P^*\in\mathcal{A}_\Gamma^+$ and write $\omega_{V_X+P}=(\omega_{V_X})_P$.
    \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Polynomial decay.} Fix $C_\mathrm{int}>0$, $\nu>0$ and $\mu>3D+\nu$. Assume that $H,V\in\mathcal{P}_\mu$, $P\in\mathcal{A}_\nu^+$ and
    \begin{align*}
        \norm H_\mu+\norm V_\mu&<C_\mathrm{int}, \qquad (1+r)^{-\nu}\leq(1+r)^{Dn_\mathrm{cor}}\zeta_\mathrm{cor}(r),\qquad r\geq0.
    \end{align*}
    Then there are a polynomial $g$ and a constant $C>0$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{V_X+P}(A)-\omega_{V_X}(A)|
        &\leq C\norm A f_\mathrm{cor}(|Y|)(1+d(x,Y))^{Dn_\mathrm{cor}}
            \zeta_\mathrm{cor}(d(x,Y)/2)\\
        &\quad\times\Bigl(\exp\bigl(g(|\beta|)(1+\norm P_{\nu,x})
                 \norm P_{\nu,x}\bigr)-1\Bigr).
    \end{align*}

    \item \textbf{Exponential decay.} Fix $a,b,c>0$ such that $b > 4a$ and $c> 8 a$, and let $C_\mathrm{int}=C_\mathrm{int}(a,c)$ be as in Proposition \ref{prop: loc of phi}. Assume that $H,V\in\mathcal{P}_{\exp(-c\cdot)}$, $P\in\mathcal{A}_{\exp(-b\cdot)}^+$ and
    \begin{align*}
        \norm H_{\exp(-c\cdot)}+\norm V_{\exp(-c\cdot)}
        &<\frac{C_\mathrm{int}}{1+|\beta|},\\
        \exp(-ar)&\leq(1+r)^{Dn_\mathrm{cor}}\zeta_\mathrm{cor}(r),\qquad r\geq0.
    \end{align*}
    Then there are constants $C$ ,$C^\prime>0$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        &|\omega_{V_X+P}(A)-\omega_{V_X}(A)|\\
        &\quad\leq C\norm A f_\mathrm{cor}(|Y|)(1+d(x,Y))^{Dn_\mathrm{cor}}
              \zeta_\mathrm{cor}(d(x,Y)/2)\\
        &\qquad\times\Bigl(\exp \bigl( C^\prime|\beta|(1+|\beta|)
              (1+\norm P_{\exp(-b\cdot),x})
              \norm P_{\exp(-b\cdot),x} \bigr)-1\Bigr).
    \end{align*}
    \end{enumerate}
    Both bounds hold for $x\in\Gamma$, $Y\in P_0(\Gamma)$ and $A\in\mathcal{A}_Y$. The coefficients of $g$ and the constants $C$ and $C^\prime$ are independent of $H$, $V$, $X$, $P$ and $\beta$ subject to the stated restrictions.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm: li from lppl}
    Let $H$, $V\in\mathcal{P}_F$ for a decay function $F$, and let $\tau$ be the dynamics generated by $\mathcal{L}_H$. Let $\beta\in\mathbb{R}$ and let $\omega$ be a $(\tau,\beta)$-KMS state. Assume that $\omega_{V_M}$ satisfies LPPL with respect to $f_\mathrm{LPPL}$, $g_\mathrm{LPPL}$ and $\zeta_\mathrm{LPPL}$, uniformly in $M\in P_0(\Gamma)$. Then
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{V_Z}(A)-\omega_{V_Y}(A)|
        &\leq \norm A f_\mathrm{LPPL}(|X|)g_\mathrm{LPPL}(3\norm V_F)
        \sum_{z\in Z\setminus Y}\zeta_\mathrm{LPPL}(d(z,X))
    \end{align*}
    for $X\subseteq Y\subsetneq Z\in P_0(\Gamma)$ and $A\in\mathcal{A}_X$.

    If $\sum_{k=0}^\infty(1+k)^{D-1}\zeta_\mathrm{LPPL}(k)<\infty$, define
    \begin{align*}
        h(r) \coloneq \sum_{k=\lceil r\rceil}^\infty(1+k)^{D-1}\zeta_\mathrm{LPPL}(k),\qquad r\geq0.
    \end{align*}
    Then
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{V_Z}(A)-\omega_{V_Y}(A)|
        &\leq C_\mathrm{sur}\norm A\,|X|f_\mathrm{LPPL}(|X|)
        g_\mathrm{LPPL}(3\norm V_F)h(d(X,Z\setminus Y))
    \end{align*}
    for the same $X$, $Y$, $Z$, $A$. Moreover, if $\omega_{sV_M}$ satisfies LPPL with the same functions uniformly in $M\in P_0(\Gamma)$ and $s\in[0,1]$, then both bounds hold with $\omega_{V_Z}$ and $\omega_{V_Y}$ replaced by $\omega_{sV_Z}$ and $\omega_{sV_Y}$, uniformly in $s$.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm: doc from LI}
    Let $H \in \mathcal{P}_F$ for some decay function $F$ and let $\tau$ be the dynamics generated by $\mathcal{L}_{H}$. Let $\beta \in \mathbb{R}$ and let $\omega$ be the tracial state\footnote{The tracial state is the $(\Tilde{\tau}, \beta)$-KMS state for the dynamics $\Tilde{\tau}$ generated by $\mathcal{L}_0$}. Assume that $\omega$ satisfies LI for the perturbation $H$ with respect to $\zeta_\mathrm{LI}$, $f_\mathrm{LI}$ and $g_\mathrm{LI}$. Assume that
    \begin{align*}
        (1 + r)^D \, F(r) \leq \zeta_\mathrm{LI} (r) \, , \quad r \geq 0 \, .
    \end{align*}
    Denote by $\omega_H$ the $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state constructed in Proposition \ref{prop: convergence from li}. It follows that there exists a constant $C > 0$ that depends only on the lattice, $F$, the LI data and $\lVert H \rVert_F$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_H(AB)-\omega_H(A)\omega_H(B)|
        &\leq C(1+|\beta|)\bigl(f_\mathrm{LI}(|X|+|Y|)+|X|\bigr)\\
        &\quad\times\zeta_\mathrm{LI}(d(X,Y)/3)\norm A\norm B,
    \end{align*}
    for all $X$, $Y \in P_0 (\Gamma)$ and $A \in \mathcal{A}_X$, $B \in \mathcal{A}_Y$.
    The same bound holds with $\omega_H$ replaced by $\omega_{H_M}$, uniformly in $M\in P_0(\Gamma)$.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm: doc from LI + doc}
    Let $\beta\in\mathbb{R}$, $c>0$ and $H$, $V\in\mathcal{P}_{\exp(-c\cdot)}$. Let $\omega$ be a $(\tau,\beta)$-KMS state, where $\tau$ is generated by $\mathcal{L}_H$. Assume that $\omega$ satisfies decay of correlations and LI for $V$ with data
    \begin{align*}
        \text{clustering:}\qquad
        \zeta_\mathrm{cor}(r)&=C_\mathrm{cor}\exp(-\mu_\mathrm{cor} r),&
        f_\mathrm{cor}(r)&=C_\mathrm{cor}\exp(C_\mathrm{cor}(1+r)),\quad n_\mathrm{cor}\in\mathbb{N}_0,\\
        \text{LI:}\qquad
        \zeta_\mathrm{LI}(r)&=C_\mathrm{LI}\exp(-\mu_\mathrm{LI} r),&
        f_\mathrm{LI}(r)&=C_\mathrm{LI}\exp(C_\mathrm{LI}(1+r)),\quad g_\mathrm{LI},
    \end{align*}
    where $C_\mathrm{cor}$, $C_\mathrm{LI} \geq 1$ and $\mu_\mathrm{cor}$, $\mu_\mathrm{LI}>0$. Let $\omega_V$ be the KMS state for $H+V$ selected by Proposition \ref{prop: convergence from li}. There are constants $C,\kappa>0$ such that, for $X$, $Y\in P_0(\Gamma)$ and $A\in\mathcal{A}_X$, $B\in\mathcal{A}_Y$, provided that at least one observable is even,
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_V(AB)-\omega_V(A)\omega_V(B)|
        &\leq C\exp\bigl(C(1+|X|+|Y|)\bigr) \, \norm A \, \norm B\\
        &\quad\times
        \exp\left(-\kappa(1+d(X,Y))^{1/((D+1)(D+2))}\right).
    \end{align*}
    The constants $C$ and $\kappa$ are independent of $X$, $Y$, $A$ or $B$.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
The proof of the preceding theorem gives the sharper support-dependent estimate
\begin{align*}
    |\omega_V(AB)-\omega_V(A)\omega_V(B)|
    &\leq  C f_\mathrm{LI} (|X|+|Y|) \,  \norm A \, \norm B \,  \exp \left( -\kappa
    \left(\frac{(1+d(X,Y))^{1/(D+2)}}{1+|X|+|Y|}\right)^{1/D}\right).
\end{align*}
Using this bound, polynomial growth of the LI support function yields super-polynomial decay with a polynomial support prefactor. Moreover, choosing polynomial growth of the support function in the clustering assumption permits a better stretched-exponential exponent. We have chosen the assumptions and the bound in the theorem because they allow exponential support dependence while keeping the statement transparent.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm: QBP SLT}
    Let $\beta\in\mathbb{R}$, $C_\mathrm{int}>0$ and $\mu$, $\nu>0$. Let $H$, $V\in\mathcal{P}_\mu$. For $s\in[0,1]$, let $\tau^{sV}$ be the dynamics generated by $\mathcal{L}_{H+sV}$. Let $\omega$ be a $(\tau^{0V},\beta)$-KMS state satisfying $V$-uniform decay of correlations with respect to
    \begin{align*}
        \zeta_\mathrm{cor}(r)=C_\mathrm{cor}(1+r)^{-m_\mathrm{cor}},\qquad f_\mathrm{cor},\qquad n_\mathrm{cor},
    \end{align*}
    for some $C_\mathrm{cor}>0$. Assume that
    \begin{align*}
        \nu>D,\qquad \mu>3D+\nu,\qquad m_\mathrm{cor}\geq\nu+Dn_\mathrm{cor},\qquad
        \norm H_\mu+\norm V_\mu<C_\mathrm{int}.
    \end{align*}
    For each $s\in[0,1]$, denote by $\omega_{sV}$ the $(\tau^{sV},\beta)$-KMS state constructed in Proposition \ref{prop: convergence from li}. Then the following holds:
    \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Continuity of local expectations.} There is a polynomial $g$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{sV}(A)-\omega_{tV}(A)|
        \leq |s-t|\,g(|\beta|)\norm V_\nu\norm A\,|Y|f_\mathrm{cor}(|Y|)
    \end{align*}
    for $Y\in P_0(\Gamma)$, $A\in\mathcal{A}_Y$ and $s,t\in[0,1]$. Its coefficients depend only on the lattice, the decay exponents, $C_\mathrm{int}$ and the common clustering data, but not on $V$ within this class.

    \item \textbf{A continuously differentiable path.} For every $A\in\mathcal{A}_0$, the map $s\mapsto\omega_{sV}(A)$ is continuously differentiable, with one-sided derivatives at the endpoints, and
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s}\omega_{sV}(A)
        =-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{x\in\Gamma}
        \omega_{sV}\bigl(\{\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{sV}}
            (V_x-\omega_{sV}(V_x)),A\}\bigr).
\end{align*}
    The sum converges absolutely and uniformly in $s\in[0,1]$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Let $A \in \mathcal{A}$ be arbitrary. Proposition \ref{prop: QBP differential equation} gives a differential equation for $s \mapsto \omega_{sP}(A)$. We construct a functional $\varphi_{sP}(A)$ by a Dyson series and show that it satisfies the same differential equation and initial condition. A Grönwall argument then identifies the two functionals.

Proposition \ref{prop: QBP differential equation} gives
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \omega_{s P} (A) = - \frac{\beta}{2} \, \omega_{s P} \bigr( \bigr\{ \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P - \omega_{sP} (P)),  \, A \bigr\} \bigr) \, .
\end{align*}
Since all involved operators are bounded, we can solve this differential equation explicitly by the Dyson series. That is, we define the operators
\begin{align*} \label{eq: eta}
    \eta_{s P} &\coloneq \mathcal{T} \exp \bigr( - \frac{\beta}{2} \int_0^s \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{\sigma P}} (P - \omega_{\sigma P} (P)) \, \mathrm{d}  \sigma \bigr) \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation}
    \\
    &\coloneq \sum_{n = 0}^\infty (- \tfrac{\beta}{2})^n \, \int_{0 \leq \sigma_{n} \leq \cdots \leq \sigma_1 \leq s} \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{ \sigma_n P}} (P - \omega_{\sigma_n P}(P)) \dots \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{ \sigma_1 P}} (P - \omega_{\sigma_1 P}(P)) \, \mathrm{d} \sigma_n \ldots \mathrm{d} \sigma_1 \, ,
\end{align*}
where $\mathcal{T}$ denotes the time-ordering operator. The adjoint operator is given by
\begin{align*}
    \eta_{s P}^* &\coloneq \overline{\mathcal{T}} \exp \bigr( - \frac{\beta}{2} \int_0^s \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{\sigma P}} (P - \omega_{\sigma P} (P)) \, \mathrm{d}  \sigma \bigr)
    \\
    &\coloneq \sum_{n = 0}^\infty (- \tfrac{\beta}{2})^n \, \int_{0 \leq \sigma_1 \leq \cdots \leq \sigma_n \leq s }\, \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{\sigma_n P}} (P - \omega_{\sigma_n P}(P)) \dots \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{\sigma_1 P}} (P - \omega_{\sigma_1 P}(P)) \, \mathrm{d} \sigma_1 \ldots \mathrm{d} \sigma_n \, ,
\end{align*}
where $\overline{\mathcal{T}}$ denotes the reverse time-ordering operator. From Lemma \ref{lem: continuity of phi} and the Dyson series it follows that the maps $s \mapsto \eta_{sP}$ and $s \mapsto \eta_{s P}^*$ are differentiable with derivative
\begin{align*} \label{eq: derivative eta}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \eta_{s P } = - \frac{\beta}{2} \,\eta_{s P} \, \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P - \omega_{s P} (P)) \, , \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation} 
\end{align*}
and 
\begin{align*} \label{eq: derivative eta*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \eta_{s P }^* = - \frac{\beta}{2} \, \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P - \omega_{s P} (P)) \,\eta_{s P}^* \, , \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation}
\end{align*}
respectively. Using the operator $\eta_{sP} \in \mathcal{A}$ we define the functional 
\begin{align*}
    \varphi_{sP} (A) \coloneq \omega(\eta_{s P} \, A \, \eta_{sP}^*) \, .
\end{align*}
We have $\varphi_{0P}=\omega$, and Equations \eqref{eq: derivative eta} and \eqref{eq: derivative eta*} give
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \varphi_{s P} (A) &= - \frac{\beta}{2} \, \omega \bigr( \eta_{s P} \, \bigr\{ \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P - \omega_{sP} (P)),  \, A \bigr\}  \, \eta_{sP}^* \bigr)
    \\
    &= - \frac{\beta}{2} \, \varphi_{sP} \bigr( \bigr\{ \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P - \omega_{sP} (P)),  \, A \bigr\} \bigr) \, .
\end{align*}
Thus, the maps $s \mapsto \omega_{s P} (A)$ and $s \mapsto \varphi_{s P} (A)$ satisfy the same initial value problem for every $A \in \mathcal{A}$. To identify the two functionals, first note that
\begin{align*}
    \lVert \eta_{sP} \rVert \leq \exp ( |\beta| \, \lVert P \rVert ) \, ,
\end{align*}
since $\lVert \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P) \rVert \leq \lVert P \rVert$. Consequently,
\begin{align*}
    |\varphi_{s P} (A)| \leq \exp(2 \, |\beta | \, \lVert P \rVert ) \, \lVert A \rVert \, , 
\end{align*}
which yields $\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\norm{\varphi_{sP}}<\infty$. Integrating the two scalar differential equations, and using $\norm{\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{sP}}(P-\omega_{sP}(P))}\leq2\norm P$, gives
\begin{align*}
    |\omega_{sP}(A)-\omega_{uP}(A)|
    &\leq 2|\beta|\norm P\norm A\,|s-u|,\\
    |\varphi_{sP}(A)-\varphi_{uP}(A)|
    &\leq 2|\beta|\norm P\mathrm{e}^{2|\beta|\norm P}\norm A\,|s-u|.
\end{align*}
Taking the supremum over $\norm A\leq1$ shows norm-continuity of both paths in $\mathcal{A}^*$. Set $\delta_{sP}=\omega_{sP}-\varphi_{sP}$. We have that $\delta_{0P}=0$. Since both functionals satisfy the same differential equation we also have 
\begin{align*}
    \delta_{sP}(A)
    &=-\frac{\beta}{2}\int_0^s
      \delta_{uP}\bigl(\{\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{uP}}(P-\omega_{uP}(P)),A\}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d} u.
\end{align*}
This yields the estimate 
\begin{align*}
    \norm{\delta_{sP}}
    &\leq2|\beta|\norm P\int_0^s\norm{\delta_{uP}}\,\mathrm{d} u.
\end{align*}
The preceding norm estimates show that the integrand is continuous. Grönwall's lemma gives $\delta_{sP}=0$, which concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
For $\beta=0$, Equation \eqref{eq: perturbed vector} gives $\Omega_{sP}=\Omega$, so $\omega_{sP}=\omega$ and the derivative is zero. Assume $\beta\neq0$.
Let $A\in\mathcal{A}$ be arbitrary. First, we assume that $P=P^*\in\mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$, and hence $P\in\mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^{\tau^{sP}}$ for all $s\in[0,1]$. The general case will then follow by approximating $P$.

Assuming entire analyticity, we show the differentiability of the map $s \mapsto \Omega_{s P}$. Here, $\Omega_{s P}$ is the vector defined in Equation \eqref{eq: perturbed vector}. The following lemma was proved in \cite[Lemma 2]{EO19} for $W^*$-dynamical systems. Their argument can also be adapted to $C^*$-dynamical systems. Here, we give a different proof based on Araki's expansionals, see \cite{A73b}.
\begin{lemma}\label{lem: derivative vector}
    Let $(\mathcal{A}, \tau)$ be a $C^*$-dynamical system, $P = P^* \in \mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$, and $s \in [0, 1]$. Furthermore, let $\omega$ be a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state and $\Omega_{s P}$ the vector on the GNS Hilbert space constructed in Equation \eqref{eq: perturbed vector}. Then the map 
    \begin{align*}
        s \mapsto \Omega_{s P}
    \end{align*}
    is differentiable with respect to the norm of $\mathcal{H}$ and the derivative is given by
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s  P} = - \beta \int_0^{1/2} \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \pi(\tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P)) \, \Omega_{s P} \, .
\end{align*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $z\in\mathbb{C}$, we define the expansional by
\begin{align*}
    E_{sP}(z)
    \coloneq\sum_{n=0}^\infty\mathrm{i}^n
    \int_{0\leq\sigma_n\leq\cdots\leq\sigma_1\leq z}
    \tau_{\sigma_n}(sP)\cdots\tau_{\sigma_1}(sP)
    \,\mathrm{d}\sigma_1\cdots\mathrm{d}\sigma_n.
\end{align*}
Here $\sigma_j=zt_j$ with $0\leq t_n\leq\cdots\leq t_1\leq1$, so the integrals are along the line segment from $0$ to $z$. Since $P$ is entire, $M_z\coloneq\sup_{0\leq t\leq1}\norm{\tau_{zt}(P)}$ is finite. The $n$-th summand and its $s$-derivative for $n \geq 1$ are bounded uniformly in $s\in[0,1]$ by
\[
    \frac{(|z|M_z)^n}{n!},
\]
and 
\[
    \frac{(|z|M_z)^n}{(n-1)!},
\]
respectively. Thus we may differentiate term by term.

In each differentiated product, let $u$ be the time of the differentiated factor. The factors with times below $u$ give $E_{sP}(u)$, while shifting the remaining times by $u$ gives $\tau_u(E_{sP}(z-u))$. Summing the absolutely convergent series yields
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s}E_{sP}(z)
    =\mathrm{i}\int_0^z E_{sP}(u)\tau_u(P)\tau_u(E_{sP}(z-u))\,\mathrm{d} u.
\end{align*}
The expansional and the perturbed dynamics satisfy
\begin{align*}
    E_{sP}(z)&=E_{sP}(u)\tau_u(E_{sP}(z-u)),\\
    \tau_u^{sP}(P)&=E_{sP}(u)\tau_u(P)E_{sP}(u)^{-1},
\end{align*}
see \cite[Proposition 5.4.1]{BR2}. Consequently,
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s}E_{sP}(z)
    &=\mathrm{i}\int_0^z\tau_u^{sP}(P)E_{sP}(z)\,\mathrm{d} u.
\end{align*}
Choosing $z=\mathrm{i}\beta/2$ and substituting $u=\mathrm{i}\beta\sigma$ gives
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s}E_{sP}\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\beta}{2}\right)
    &=-\beta\int_0^{1/2}\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta\sigma}^{sP}(P)
        E_{sP}\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\beta}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma.
\end{align*}
By \cite[Corollary 5.4.5]{BR2}, $\Omega_{sP}=\pi(E_{sP}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2))\Omega$. Boundedness of $\pi$ therefore gives
\[
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s}\Omega_{sP}
    =-\beta\int_0^{1/2}\pi(\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta\sigma}^{sP}(P))
        \Omega_{sP}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma. \qedhere
\]

\end{proof}


The preceding lemma gives the derivative of $s \mapsto \omega_{sP}(A)$.


\medskip
\begin{lemma} \label{lem: Derivative of state}
    Let $(\mathcal{A}, \tau)$ be a $C^*$-dynamical system, $P = P^* \in \mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$, and $s \in [0, 1]$. Furthermore, let $\omega$ be a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state and $\Omega_{s P}$ the vector on the GNS Hilbert space constructed in Equation \eqref{eq: perturbed vector} and $\omega_{s P}$ the state constructed in Equation \eqref{eq: perturbed state}. Then the map
    \begin{align*}
        s \mapsto \omega_{sP} (A)
    \end{align*}
    is differentiable for every $A \in \mathcal{A}$. Moreover, the derivative is given by
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \omega_{sP} (A) = - \beta \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \bigr( \omega_{sP} (A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P)) - \omega_{sP} (A) \, \omega_{sP} (P) \bigr) \, .
    \end{align*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $A \in \mathcal{A}$. Differentiating the state in the GNS representation gives
    \begin{align*} \label{eq: derivative of KMS}
        \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \omega_{sP} (A) &= \frac{(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P}, \, \pi(A) \, \Omega_{s P} ) + (\Omega_{s P}, \, \pi(A) \, \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P} ) }{(\Omega_{s P}, \, \Omega_{s P})} 
        \\
        & \quad- \omega_{sP} (A) \, \frac{(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P}, \, \Omega_{s P} ) + (\Omega_{s P}, \, \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P} ) }{(\Omega_{s P}, \, \Omega_{s P})} \, . \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation}
    \end{align*}
    We focus on the first term, where we use Lemma \ref{lem: derivative vector} and $P \in \mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^{\tau^{s P}}$ to calculate
    \begin{align*}
        \hspace{4ex} & \hspace{-4ex} \frac{(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P}, \, \pi(A) \, \Omega_{s P} ) + (\Omega_{s P}, \, \pi(A) \, \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P} ) }{(\Omega_{s P}, \, \Omega_{s P})} 
        \\
        &= - \beta \int_0^{1/2} \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \bigr( \omega_{s P} ( \tau_{- \mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) \, A) + \omega_{s P} ( A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) ) \bigr)
        \\
        & = - \beta \int_0^{1/2} \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \omega_{s P} ( A \,\tau_{ \mathrm{i} (1 - \sigma) \beta}^{s P} (P) + A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) )
        \\
        & = - \beta \int_0^{1} \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \omega_{s P} ( A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P)) \, .
    \end{align*}
    In the second equality we used the KMS condition and the group property
    \begin{align*}
        \tau_z(\tau_w(P))=\tau_{z+w}(P),\qquad
        P\in\mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^{\tau^{sP}},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{C}.
    \end{align*} The third equality follows by a change of integration variable in the first summand. The second term in Equation \eqref{eq: derivative of KMS} can be obtained by choosing $A = 1$. Combining both statements concludes the proof.
\end{proof}


We use Lemma \ref{lem: Derivative of state} and the functional calculus to prove Equation \eqref{eq: QBP differential eqtn. prop}. By the lemma,
\begin{align*} \label{eq: deriv state proof qbp}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \omega_{s P} (A) = - \beta \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \bigr( \omega_{sP} (A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P)) - \omega_{sP} (A) \, \omega_{sP} (P) \bigr)  \, . \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation}
\end{align*}
We first deal with the first summand, the second summand will follow by normalization. Let $(\mathcal{H}_s, \, \pi_s, \, \Omega_s)$ be the GNS triple associated to $\omega_{s P}$, and $H_{s}$ the Hamiltonian that implements the group of automorphisms $\tau^{s P}$ in this representation. Note that by stationarity of $\omega_{s P}$ the GNS vector $\Omega_s$ is an eigenvector of the GNS Hamiltonian with eigenvalue zero. Passing to the GNS representation gives
\begin{align*}
    - \beta \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \omega_{s P} ( A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) ) = - \beta  \, ( \Omega_s, \, \pi_s(A) \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \mathrm{e}^{- \beta \sigma H_{s}} \, \pi_s ( P ) \, \Omega_s ) \, ,
\end{align*}
where we have used Lemma \ref{lem: impl is entire}. Inserting the identity $(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_s})(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_s})^{-1}$ gives
\begin{align*}
    \hspace{4ex} & \hspace{-4ex} - \beta \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \omega_{s P} ( A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) ) 
    \\
    &= - \beta  \, ( \Omega_s, \, \pi_s(A)\, (1 + \mathrm{e}^{- \beta H_{s}}) \, (1 + \mathrm{e}^{- \beta H_{s}})^{-1} \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \mathrm{e}^{- \beta \sigma H_{s}} \, \pi_s ( P )   \, \Omega_s ) \, . \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation} \label{eq: proof qbp}
\end{align*}
This expression is well-defined by Lemma \ref{lem: impl is entire}, since $\pi_s(P)\Omega_s$ belongs to the domain of $\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_s}$. Functional calculus gives
\begin{align*}
    (1+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_s})\hat f_\beta(H_s)\pi_s(P)\Omega_s
    =2\int_0^1\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\sigma H_s}\pi_s(P)\Omega_s\,\mathrm{d}\sigma.
\end{align*}
Hence, we can rewrite the integral in Equation \eqref{eq: proof qbp} as
\begin{align*}
    - \beta \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \omega_{s P} ( A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) )& = - \frac{\beta}{2}  \, ( \Omega_s, \, \pi_s(A) \, (1 + \mathrm{e}^{- \beta H_{s}}) \, \hat{f}_\beta (H_s) \, \pi_s(P) \, \Omega_s) \, ,
\end{align*}
where we used the function $\hat{f}_\beta (x)$ defined in Equation \eqref{def: f_hat}. Using its Fourier transform gives
\begin{align*}
    - \beta \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \omega_{s P} ( A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) ) &= - \frac{\beta}{2} \, \int_\mathbb{R} \mathrm{d} t \, f_\beta(t) \, ( \Omega_s, \, \pi_s(A) \, (1 + \mathrm{e}^{- \beta H_s}) \, \mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i} t H_s} \, \pi_s ( P ) \, \Omega_s ) 
    \\
    &= - \frac{\beta}{2} \, \int_\mathbb{R} \mathrm{d} t \, f_\beta(t) \, ( \Omega_s, \, \pi_s(A) \, (\mathrm{e}^{- \mathrm{i} t H_s} + \mathrm{e}^{- (\mathrm{i} t + \beta) H_s}) \, \pi_s ( P ) \, \Omega_s ) \, ,
\end{align*}
The interchange with the unbounded operator is justified as follows. Since $P$ is entire, $\pi_s(P)\Omega_s$ belongs to the domain of $\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_s}$, and
\begin{align*}
    &\int_\mathbb{R} f_\beta(t)
       \norm{(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_s})\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i} tH_s}\pi_s(P)\Omega_s}\,\mathrm{d} t\\
    &\quad\leq
      \norm{\pi_s(P)\Omega_s}
       +\norm{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_s}\pi_s(P)\Omega_s}<\infty.
\end{align*}
The integral thus converges in the graph norm of the closed operator
$1+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H_s}$, which permits moving this operator through the integral. Using Lemma \ref{lem: impl is entire} we can go back to the state to get
\begin{align*}
    - \beta \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \omega_{s P} ( A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) ) &= - \frac{\beta}{2}  \, \int_\mathbb{R} \mathrm{d} t \, f_\beta(t) \, \omega_{s P} ( A \, \tau_{-t}^{s P} (P) +  A  \, \tau_{- t + \mathrm{i} \beta}^{s P} (P) ) \, .
\end{align*}
The KMS condition gives
\begin{align*}
    - \beta \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \omega_{s P} ( A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) ) &= - \frac{\beta}{2}  \,  \int_\mathbb{R} \mathrm{d} t \, f_\beta(t) \, \omega_{s P} ( \{ \tau_{-t}^{s P} (P),  \, A \} ) 
    \\
    &= - \frac{\beta}{2} \, \omega_{s P} ( \{ \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P ),  \, A \} ) \, .
\end{align*}
Substitution into Equation \eqref{eq: deriv state proof qbp} gives
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \omega_{s P} (A) = - \frac{\beta}{2} \, \omega_{s P} \bigr( \bigr\{ \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P - \omega_{s P} (P)),  \, A \bigr\} \bigr) \, ,
\end{align*}
where we have used that $f_\beta$ is normalized. This proves the proposition for $P=P^*\in\mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$ and every $A\in\mathcal{A}$.

It remains to prove the differential equation for an arbitrary perturbation $P=P^*\in\mathcal{A}$. Since $\mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$ is dense in $\mathcal{A}$, choose $P_n=P_n^*\in\mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$ with $P_n\to P$. It suffices to show that the derivatives of $s\mapsto\omega_{sP_n}(A)$ converge uniformly in $s\in[0,1]$ to the derivative in the statement. We use
\begin{align*}
    \omega_{s P_n} (A) &= \omega_{s P + s(P_n - P)} (A)
    \\
    & = (\omega_{s P})_{s(P_n - P)} (A) \, .
\end{align*}
By \cite[Theorem 5.4.4.(3)]{BR2},  $Q \mapsto \omega_Q$ is norm-continuous for bounded self-adjoint perturbations. This gives
\begin{align*}
    \sup_{s\in[0,1]}\norm{\omega_{sP_n}-\omega_{sP}}\longrightarrow0.
\end{align*}
Indeed, otherwise there would be a subsequence and $s_j\to s$ for which the differences stayed bounded away from zero. Since
\begin{align*}
    \norm{s_jP_{n_j}-sP}
    &\leq\norm{P_{n_j}-P}+|s_j-s|\norm P\longrightarrow0,
\end{align*}
norm continuity gives both $\omega_{s_jP_{n_j}}\to\omega_{sP}$ and $\omega_{s_jP}\to\omega_{sP}$, which is a contradiction. It remains to show that
\begin{align*}
    \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{ s P_n}} (P_n) = \int_\mathbb{R} \mathrm{d} t\, f_\beta (t) \, \tau_{-t}^{s P_n} (P_n) \, ,
\end{align*}    
    converges to $\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P)$ uniformly in $s \in [0,1]$. Adding and subtracting $\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{sP_n}}(P)$ gives
\begin{align*}
    \lVert \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P_n}} (P_n)  - \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P) \rVert &\leq \lVert \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P_n}} (P_n) - \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P_n}} (P) \rVert  + \lVert \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P_n}} (P) - \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P) \rVert \, .
\end{align*}
    For the first term, linearity and the normalization of $f_\beta$ give
\begin{align*}
    \lVert \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P_n}} (P_n) - \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P_n}} (P) \rVert \leq \lVert P_n - P \rVert \, , 
\end{align*}
    which converges to zero uniformly in $s \in [0, 1]$. For the second term, Duhamel's formula gives, for every $t\in\mathbb{R}$,
    \[
        \sup_{s\in[0,1]}
        \norm{\tau_t^{sP_n}(P)-\tau_t^{sP}(P)}
        \leq 2|t|\norm{P_n-P}\norm P.
    \]
    This yields
    \[
        \sup_{s\in[0,1]}
        \norm{\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{sP_n}}(P)-
        \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{sP}}(P)}
        \leq 2\norm{P_n-P}\norm P
        \int_\mathbb{R} |t|f_\beta(t)\,\mathrm{d} t
        \longrightarrow0.
    \]
    This proves the required convergence uniformly in $s\in[0,1]$.

Together, the preceding estimates show that
\begin{align*}
    \omega_{s P_n} \bigr( \bigr\{ \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P_n}} (P_n - \omega_{s P_n} (P_n)),  \, A \bigr\} \bigr)
\end{align*}
converges to
\begin{align*}
    \omega_{s P} \bigr( \bigr\{ \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P - \omega_{s P} (P)),  \, A \bigr\} \bigr)
\end{align*}
uniformly in $s \in [0, 1]$. Therefore, the map $s \mapsto \omega_{s P} (A)$ is differentiable with derivative
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \omega_{s P} (A) = - \frac{\beta}{2} \omega_{s P} \bigr( \bigr\{ \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P - \omega_{s P} (P)),  \, A \bigr\} \bigr) \, ,
\end{align*}
which concludes the proof of the proposition.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem: derivative vector}
    Let $(\mathcal{A}, \tau)$ be a $C^*$-dynamical system, $P = P^* \in \mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$, and $s \in [0, 1]$. Furthermore, let $\omega$ be a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state and $\Omega_{s P}$ the vector on the GNS Hilbert space constructed in Equation \eqref{eq: perturbed vector}. Then the map 
    \begin{align*}
        s \mapsto \Omega_{s P}
    \end{align*}
    is differentiable with respect to the norm of $\mathcal{H}$ and the derivative is given by
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s  P} = - \beta \int_0^{1/2} \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \pi(\tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P)) \, \Omega_{s P} \, .
\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
For $z\in\mathbb{C}$, we define the expansional by
\begin{align*}
    E_{sP}(z)
    \coloneq\sum_{n=0}^\infty\mathrm{i}^n
    \int_{0\leq\sigma_n\leq\cdots\leq\sigma_1\leq z}
    \tau_{\sigma_n}(sP)\cdots\tau_{\sigma_1}(sP)
    \,\mathrm{d}\sigma_1\cdots\mathrm{d}\sigma_n.
\end{align*}
Here $\sigma_j=zt_j$ with $0\leq t_n\leq\cdots\leq t_1\leq1$, so the integrals are along the line segment from $0$ to $z$. Since $P$ is entire, $M_z\coloneq\sup_{0\leq t\leq1}\norm{\tau_{zt}(P)}$ is finite. The $n$-th summand and its $s$-derivative for $n \geq 1$ are bounded uniformly in $s\in[0,1]$ by
\[
    \frac{(|z|M_z)^n}{n!},
\]
and 
\[
    \frac{(|z|M_z)^n}{(n-1)!},
\]
respectively. Thus we may differentiate term by term.

In each differentiated product, let $u$ be the time of the differentiated factor. The factors with times below $u$ give $E_{sP}(u)$, while shifting the remaining times by $u$ gives $\tau_u(E_{sP}(z-u))$. Summing the absolutely convergent series yields
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s}E_{sP}(z)
    =\mathrm{i}\int_0^z E_{sP}(u)\tau_u(P)\tau_u(E_{sP}(z-u))\,\mathrm{d} u.
\end{align*}
The expansional and the perturbed dynamics satisfy
\begin{align*}
    E_{sP}(z)&=E_{sP}(u)\tau_u(E_{sP}(z-u)),\\
    \tau_u^{sP}(P)&=E_{sP}(u)\tau_u(P)E_{sP}(u)^{-1},
\end{align*}
see \cite[Proposition 5.4.1]{BR2}. Consequently,
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s}E_{sP}(z)
    &=\mathrm{i}\int_0^z\tau_u^{sP}(P)E_{sP}(z)\,\mathrm{d} u.
\end{align*}
Choosing $z=\mathrm{i}\beta/2$ and substituting $u=\mathrm{i}\beta\sigma$ gives
\begin{align*}
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s}E_{sP}\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\beta}{2}\right)
    &=-\beta\int_0^{1/2}\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta\sigma}^{sP}(P)
        E_{sP}\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\beta}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma.
\end{align*}
By \cite[Corollary 5.4.5]{BR2}, $\Omega_{sP}=\pi(E_{sP}(\mathrm{i}\beta/2))\Omega$. Boundedness of $\pi$ therefore gives
\[
    \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s}\Omega_{sP}
    =-\beta\int_0^{1/2}\pi(\tau_{\mathrm{i}\beta\sigma}^{sP}(P))
        \Omega_{sP}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma. \qedhere
\]
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem: Derivative of state}
    Let $(\mathcal{A}, \tau)$ be a $C^*$-dynamical system, $P = P^* \in \mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^\tau$, and $s \in [0, 1]$. Furthermore, let $\omega$ be a $(\tau, \beta)$-KMS state and $\Omega_{s P}$ the vector on the GNS Hilbert space constructed in Equation \eqref{eq: perturbed vector} and $\omega_{s P}$ the state constructed in Equation \eqref{eq: perturbed state}. Then the map
    \begin{align*}
        s \mapsto \omega_{sP} (A)
    \end{align*}
    is differentiable for every $A \in \mathcal{A}$. Moreover, the derivative is given by
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \omega_{sP} (A) = - \beta \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \bigr( \omega_{sP} (A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P)) - \omega_{sP} (A) \, \omega_{sP} (P) \bigr) \, .
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Let $A \in \mathcal{A}$. Differentiating the state in the GNS representation gives
    \begin{align*} \label{eq: derivative of KMS}
        \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \omega_{sP} (A) &= \frac{(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P}, \, \pi(A) \, \Omega_{s P} ) + (\Omega_{s P}, \, \pi(A) \, \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P} ) }{(\Omega_{s P}, \, \Omega_{s P})} 
        \\
        & \quad- \omega_{sP} (A) \, \frac{(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P}, \, \Omega_{s P} ) + (\Omega_{s P}, \, \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P} ) }{(\Omega_{s P}, \, \Omega_{s P})} \, . \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation}
    \end{align*}
    We focus on the first term, where we use Lemma \ref{lem: derivative vector} and $P \in \mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^{\tau^{s P}}$ to calculate
    \begin{align*}
        \hspace{4ex} & \hspace{-4ex} \frac{(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P}, \, \pi(A) \, \Omega_{s P} ) + (\Omega_{s P}, \, \pi(A) \, \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \Omega_{s P} ) }{(\Omega_{s P}, \, \Omega_{s P})} 
        \\
        &= - \beta \int_0^{1/2} \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \bigr( \omega_{s P} ( \tau_{- \mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) \, A) + \omega_{s P} ( A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) ) \bigr)
        \\
        & = - \beta \int_0^{1/2} \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \omega_{s P} ( A \,\tau_{ \mathrm{i} (1 - \sigma) \beta}^{s P} (P) + A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P) )
        \\
        & = - \beta \int_0^{1} \mathrm{d} \sigma \, \omega_{s P} ( A \, \tau_{\mathrm{i} \sigma \beta}^{s P} (P)) \, .
    \end{align*}
    In the second equality we used the KMS condition and the group property
    \begin{align*}
        \tau_z(\tau_w(P))=\tau_{z+w}(P),\qquad
        P\in\mathcal{A}_\mathrm{ent}^{\tau^{sP}},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{C}.
    \end{align*} The third equality follows by a change of integration variable in the first summand. The second term in Equation \eqref{eq: derivative of KMS} can be obtained by choosing $A = 1$. Combining both statements concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
We set $F(r)=(1+r)^{-\nu}$ in the polynomial regime and $F(r)=\exp(-ar)$ in the exponential regime.
    Let $A \in \mathcal{A}_Y$ be arbitrary. We start by writing the difference of expectation values as an integral
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_P (A) - \omega(A)| = \bigl| \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} s \, \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \omega_{s P} (A) \bigr| \, .
    \end{align*}
    We use the triangle inequality to move the absolute value inside the integral and Proposition \ref{prop: QBP differential equation} to evaluate the derivative
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_P (A) - \omega(A) | &\leq \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} s \, \bigl| \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} s} \omega_{s P} (A) \bigr|
        \\
        & = \frac{|\beta|}{2} \int_0^1 \mathrm{d} s \, \bigr| \omega_{s P} \bigr( \bigr\{ \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P) - \omega_{s P} \bigl( \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P) \bigr),  \, A \bigr\} \bigr) \bigr| \, ,
    \end{align*}
    where $\tau^{s P}$ is the dynamics generated by $\mathcal{L}_{H} + \mathcal{L}_{s P}$. By Lemma \ref{lem: dynamics is even} $\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{sP}}(P)$ is even. Hence, we can apply decay of correlations in the form of Lemma \ref{lem: decay of correlations for A_infty} and estimate
    \begin{align*} 
        |\omega_P (A) - \omega(A) | &\leq C \, |\beta| \, \lVert A \rVert \, \sup_{s \in [0, 1]} \, \bigl\lVert \varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P) \bigr\rVert_{F, x} \, f_\mathrm{cor}(|Y|) \, (1 + d (x, Y))^{D \, n_\mathrm{cor}} \zeta_\mathrm{cor}( d(x, Y) / 2 ) \, . 
    \end{align*}
    Bounding the decay norm of $\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{s P}} (P)$ using Proposition \ref{prop: loc of phi} yields the claim.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
We set $F(r)=(1+r)^{-\nu}$ in the polynomial regime and $F(r)=\exp(-ar)$ in the exponential regime.
    Let $A \in \mathcal{A}_Y$ be arbitrary. We use Theorem \ref{thm: QBP} to find an operator $\eta_P \in \mathcal{A}$. This operator is even, as can be seen by Equation \eqref{eq: eta}. We write
    \begin{align*}
        \omega_{V_X + P} (A) = \omega_{V_X}(\eta_P \, A \, \eta_P^*) \, .
    \end{align*}
    We use this expression to estimate the difference
    \begin{align*} \label{eq: difference LPPL}
        |\omega_{V_X + P} (A) - \omega_{V_X} (A) | &= |\omega_{V_X} (\eta_P \, A \, \eta_P^*) - \omega_{V_X} (A)|
        \\
        & \leq \bigl| \omega_{V_X} ([\eta_P, A] \, \eta_P^*) \bigr| + \bigl| \omega_{V_X} (A \, \eta_P \eta_P^*) - \omega_{V_X} (A) \, \omega_{V_X} (\eta_P \eta_P^*) \bigr| \, , \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation}
    \end{align*}
    where we have inserted a zero and used the triangle inequality. Using that $\omega_{V_X}$ is a state we bound the first term by
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{V_X} ([\eta_P, A] \, \eta_P^*)| &\leq \lVert [\eta_P, \, A] \rVert \, \lVert \eta_P^* \rVert = \lVert [\eta_P - 1, A] \rVert \, \lVert \eta_P^* \rVert \, .
    \end{align*}
    Using Lemma \ref{lem:commutator bound} we can estimate the norm of the commutator by
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert [\eta_P, A] \rVert \leq \frac{2}{F (1)} \, F(d (x, Y)) \, \lVert \eta_P - 1 \rVert_{F, x} \, \lVert A \rVert \, ,
    \end{align*}
    for any $x \in \Gamma$. Furthermore, we use the trivial bound
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert \eta_P^* \rVert = \lVert \eta_P \rVert \leq \lVert \eta_P \rVert_{F, x} \, .
    \end{align*}
    We turn to the second term in Equation \eqref{eq: difference LPPL}. Using Lemma \ref{lem: dynamics is even} we can see that $\eta_P$ is even. Hence, we can apply our assumptions on decay of correlations in the form of Lemma \ref{lem: decay of correlations for A_infty} of the initial state and find
    \begin{align*}
        \hspace{4ex} & \hspace{-4ex} |\omega_{V_X} (A \, \eta_P \eta_P^*) - \omega_{V_X} (A) \, \omega_{V_X}(\eta_P \eta_P^*)| = |\omega_{V_X} (A \, (\eta_P \eta_P^* - 1)) - \omega_{V_X} (A) \, \omega_{V_X}(\eta_P \eta_P^* - 1)|
        \\
        &\leq C \, \lVert A \rVert \, \lVert \eta_P \eta_P^* - 1 \rVert_{F,x} \, f_\mathrm{cor} (|Y|) \, (1 + d (x, Y))^{D \, n_\mathrm{cor}} \zeta_\mathrm{cor}(d (x, Y) / 2) \, ,
    \end{align*}
    for some constant $C > 0$. Combining both bounds yields
    \begin{align*}
        &|\omega_{V_X + P} (A) - \omega_{V_X} (A) |
        \\
        & \quad\leq C^\prime \, \lVert A \rVert \, \bigl( \lVert \eta_P - 1 \rVert_{F, x} \, \lVert \eta_P \rVert_{F, x} + \lVert \eta_P \eta_P^* - 1 \rVert_{F, x} \bigr) \, f_\mathrm{cor}(|Y|) \, (1 + d (x, Y))^{D \, n_\mathrm{cor}} \zeta_\mathrm{cor}(d (x, Y) / 2) \, .
    \end{align*}
    We find that the generator is bounded by
    \begin{align*}
        \norm{\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{V_X+sP}}(P)-\omega_{V_X+sP}(P)}_{F,x}\leq 2\norm{\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{V_X+sP}}(P)}_{F,x}.
    \end{align*}
    By definition of $\eta_P$ and Lemma \ref{lem: continuity decay norm} we find that 
    \begin{align*}
        \norm{\eta_P-1}_{F,x} \leq \exp \bigl( 2 |\beta|\sup_{s\in[0,1]} \norm{\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{V_X+sP}}(P)}_{F,x} \bigr) - 1.
    \end{align*}
    Using $\eta_P \eta_P^* - 1 = (\eta_P - 1) \eta_P^*+ (\eta_P^* - 1)$ and again Lemma \ref{lem: continuity decay norm} we find that
    \begin{align*}
        \norm{\eta_P\eta_P^*-1}_{F,x}
        &\leq3 \bigl( \exp\bigl(4|\beta|\sup_{s\in[0,1]} \norm{\varPhi_\beta^{\tau^{V_X+sP}}(P)}_{F,x}\bigr)-1 \bigr).
    \end{align*}
    Inserting these bounds and applying Proposition \ref{prop: loc of phi} proves the statement.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Let $X \subseteq Y \subsetneq Z\in P_0 (\Gamma)$ and $A \in \mathcal{A}_X$ be arbitrary. We first show the bound for
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{V_Z} (A) - \omega_{V_Y} (A)| \, .
    \end{align*}
    We remove the sites of $Z\setminus Y$ one at a time and use LPPL in each of the states before a site is removed.
    
    To formalize this idea, we set $N \coloneq |Z \setminus Y|$ and label the sites in $Z \setminus Y$ by $z_i$ with $i \in \{1, \dots, N\}$. We define $V_Y^i \coloneq V_{Y \cup \{z_1, \ldots, z_i\}}$ for $i=0,\ldots,N$, with $V_Y^0=V_Y$. A telescoping series gives us that 
    \begin{align*} \label{eq: sum LI}
        |\omega_{V_Z} (A) - \omega_{V_Y} (A)| \leq \sum_{i = 1}^N |\omega_{V_Y^i} (A) - \omega_{V_Y^{i - 1}} (A)| \, . \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation}
    \end{align*}
    Composition of bounded perturbations gives
    \begin{align*}
        \omega_{V_Y^{i-1}}
        =(\omega_{V_Y^i})_{V_Y^{i-1}-V_Y^i}.
    \end{align*}
    Applying LPPL to the initial state $\omega_{V_Y^i}$ therefore gives
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{V_Y^i} (A) - \omega_{V_Y^{i - 1}} (A)| \leq \lVert A \rVert \, f_\mathrm{LPPL} (|X|) \, g_\mathrm{LPPL}(\lVert V_Y^i - V_Y^{i - 1} \rVert_{F, z_i} ) \, \zeta_\mathrm{LPPL} (d (z_i, X) ) \, .
    \end{align*}
    We bound the decay norm of the interaction by
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert V_Y^i - V_Y^{i - 1} \rVert_{F, z_i} \leq \sum_{\substack{\Lambda \subseteq Y \cup \{z_1, \ldots, z_i \} \\ \Lambda \nsubseteq Y \cup \{z_1, \ldots, z_{i - 1} \}}} \lVert V(\Lambda) \rVert_{F, z_i} \, .
    \end{align*}
    The bound 
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert V(\Lambda) \rVert_{F, z_i} \leq 3 \frac{\lVert V (\Lambda) \rVert}{F(\mathrm{diam} (\Lambda))}
    \end{align*}
    then yields the bound 
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert V_Y^i - V_Y^{i - 1} \rVert_{F, z_i} \leq 3 \lVert V \rVert_{F} \, ,
    \end{align*}
    and hence
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{V_Y^i} (A) - \omega_{V_Y^{i - 1}} (A)| \leq \lVert A \rVert \, f_\mathrm{LPPL} (|X|) \, g_\mathrm{LPPL}(3 \lVert V \rVert_{F} ) \, \zeta_\mathrm{LPPL} (d (z_i, X) ) \, .
    \end{align*}
    Inserting this bound into Equation \eqref{eq: sum LI} gives
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{V_Z}(A)-\omega_{V_Y}(A)|
        &\leq\norm A f_\mathrm{LPPL}(|X|)g_\mathrm{LPPL}(3\norm V_F)
                 \sum_{z\in Z\setminus Y}\zeta_\mathrm{LPPL}(d(z,X))
        \\
        & \leq C_\mathrm{sur} \, |X| \, \norm A f_\mathrm{LPPL}(|X|)g_\mathrm{LPPL}(3\norm V_F) \, h(d(X, Z \setminus Y)).
    \end{align*}
    The first bound uses LPPL only in the states $\omega_{V_Y^i}$, $i=1,\ldots,N$; these form the stated sequence when read in reverse order. The second bound uses the summability assumption. For the uniform assertion, apply these estimates to $sV$ and use
    \[
        g_\mathrm{LPPL}(3\norm{sV}_F)
        =g_\mathrm{LPPL}(3s\norm V_F)
        \leq g_\mathrm{LPPL}(3\norm V_F). \qedhere
    \]
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Let $X$, $Y \in P_0 (\Gamma)$ and $A \in \mathcal{A}_X$, $B\in \mathcal{A}_Y$ be arbitrary. We fix $r = d(X, Y) / 3$ for the rest of the proof. If $r\leq1$, then $\zeta_\mathrm{LI}(r)\geq\zeta_\mathrm{LI}(1)\geq2^DF(1)>0$, and
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_H(AB)-\omega_H(A)\omega_H(B)|
        \leq\frac{2}{\zeta_\mathrm{LI}(1)}
        \bigl(f_\mathrm{LI}(|X|+|Y|)+|X|\bigr)\zeta_\mathrm{LI}(r)\norm A\norm B.
    \end{align*}
    The same estimate holds for $\omega_{H_M}$ for every $M\in P_0(\Gamma)$. We therefore assume $r>1$, so the enlarged supports are disjoint. We first prove the estimate for the finite-patch states. Fix $M\in P_0(\Gamma)$ with $X\cup Y\subseteq M$. Throughout this estimate, $X_r$ and $Y_r$ denote the fattenings intersected with $M$. By LI we may restrict the perturbation to $X_r\cup Y_r$. We then remove the terms connecting the two fattenings; the remaining state has zero covariance by the product property of the tracial state.

    If $X_r\cup Y_r=X\cup Y$, then $d(X,M\setminus X)\geq r$. Corollary \ref{cor: cont. KMS state} and the product property of the tracial state (see \cite[Proposition 4.6]{AM03}) give
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{H_M}(AB)-\omega_{H_M}(A)\omega_{H_M}(B)|
        &\leq 3|\beta|\norm A\norm B
            \norm{H_M-H_X-H_{M\setminus X}}\\
        &\leq 3|\beta|\norm A\norm B
            \sum_{x\in X}\sum_{\substack{x\in Z\subseteq M\\\mathrm{diam} Z\geq r}}\norm{H(Z)}\\
        &\leq 3|\beta|\,|X|F(r)\norm H_F\norm A\norm B.
    \end{align*}
    This proves the bound in this case, since $F(r)\leq\zeta_\mathrm{LI}(r)$. We may therefore assume that $X\cup Y\subsetneq X_r\cup Y_r$.
    If $X_r\cup Y_r=M$, the following difference vanishes; otherwise, LI gives
    \begin{align*}
        \hspace{4ex}&\hspace{-4ex} |\omega_{H_M} (A B) - \omega_{H_{X_r \cup Y_r}} (A B) - \bigl( \omega_{H_M} (A) \, \omega_{H_M} (B) - \omega_{H_{X_r \cup Y_r}} (A) \, \omega_{H_{X_r \cup Y_r}} (B) \bigr) | 
        \\
        &\leq 3 \, \lVert A \rVert \, \lVert B \rVert \, f_\mathrm{LI} (|X| + |Y|) \, g_\mathrm{LI} (\lVert H \rVert_F) \, \zeta_\mathrm{LI} (r) \, . 
    \end{align*}
    We use Corollary \ref{cor: cont. KMS state} and find
    \begin{gather*}
        | \omega_{H_{X_r \cup Y_r}} (A B) - \omega_{H_{X_r} + H_{Y_r}} (A B) - \bigl( \omega_{H_{X_r \cup Y_r}} (A) \, \omega_{H_{X_r \cup Y_r}} (B) - \omega_{H_{X_r} + H_{Y_r}} (A) \, \omega_{H_{X_r} + H_{Y_r}} (B) \bigr) | 
        \\
        \leq 3 |\beta| \, \lVert A \rVert \, \lVert B \rVert \, \lVert \sum_{ \substack{ Z\subseteq X_r \cup Y_r \\ Z \nsubseteq X_r, \, Z \nsubseteq Y_r}} H(Z) \rVert \,  .  \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation} \label{eq: proof doc interaction norm}
    \end{gather*}
    Since $d(X_r,Y_r)\geq d(X,Y)-2r=r$, every interaction term in the sum meets both fattenings and has diameter at least $r$. Hence,
    \begin{align*}
        \norm{\sum_{\substack{Z\subseteq X_r\cup Y_r\\
        Z\nsubseteq X_r,\;Z\nsubseteq Y_r}}H(Z)}
        &\leq\sum_{x\in X_r}
        \sum_{\substack{x\in Z\in P_0(\Gamma)\\\mathrm{diam} Z\geq r}}\norm{H(Z)}\\
        &\leq |X_r|F(r)\norm H_F.
    \end{align*}
    Inserting this estimate into Equation \eqref{eq: proof doc interaction norm} yields
    \begin{gather*}
        | \omega_{H_{X_r \cup Y_r}} (A B) - \omega_{H_{X_r} + H_{Y_r}} (A B) - \bigl( \omega_{H_{X_r \cup Y_r}} (A) \, \omega_{H_{X_r \cup Y_r}} (B) - \omega_{H_{X_r} + H_{Y_r}} (A) \, \omega_{H_{X_r} + H_{Y_r}} (B) \bigr) | 
        \\
        \leq 3 |\beta| \, F(r) \,C_\mathrm{vol}  (1 + r)^D |X| \, \lVert A \rVert \, \lVert B \rVert \, \lVert  H \rVert_F  \, .
    \end{gather*}
    Finally, we notice that by the product property of the tracial state
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{H_{X_r} + H_{Y_r}} (A B) -  \omega_{H_{X_r} + H_{Y_r}} (A) \, \omega_{H_{X_r} + H_{Y_r}} (B)| = 0 \, .
    \end{align*}
    Combining the preceding estimates gives
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{H_M} (A B) - \omega_{H_M} (A) \, \omega_{H_M} (B)| \leq  C \, (1 + |\beta|) \, \bigl( f_\mathrm{LI}(|X| + |Y|) + |X| \bigr) \, \zeta_\mathrm{LI} (r) \, \lVert A \rVert \, \lVert B \rVert \,  ,
    \end{align*}
    for some constant $C > 0$ independent of $M$. This proves the bound whenever $X\cup Y\subseteq M$.

    Let now $X$ and $Y$ be disjoint but not necessarily contained in $M$. Since $H_M\in\mathcal{A}_M$, the defining property of the conditional expectation gives $\omega_{H_M}=\omega_{H_M}\circ\mathbb{E}_M$. Moreover, the product property of the tracial state gives $\mathbb{E}_M(AB)=\mathbb{E}_M(A)\mathbb{E}_M(B)$. Therefore,
    \begin{align*}
        &\omega_{H_M}(AB)-\omega_{H_M}(A)\omega_{H_M}(B) =\omega_{H_M}(\mathbb{E}_M(A)\mathbb{E}_M(B))
            -\omega_{H_M}(\mathbb{E}_M(A))\omega_{H_M}(\mathbb{E}_M(B)).
    \end{align*}
    If one of the intersections $X\cap M$ and $Y\cap M$ is empty, the right-hand side vanishes. Otherwise, $\mathbb{E}_M(A)\in\mathcal{A}_{X\cap M}$ and $\mathbb{E}_M(B)\in\mathcal{A}_{Y\cap M}$, and contractivity of $\mathbb{E}_M$ gives the same bound. Overlapping supports are covered by the trivial covariance bound. Finally, choosing $M=\Lambda_n$ for an IAS and using Proposition \ref{prop: convergence from li} proves the bound for $\omega_H$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Let $X$, $Y\in P_0(\Gamma)$ and $A\in\mathcal{A}_X$, $B\in\mathcal{A}_Y$ be arbitrary, with at least one of $A$, $B$ even. For $\beta=0$ the state and all its perturbations are the tracial state. Hence, the statement becomes trivial. Assume $\beta \neq 0$.

    The proof proceeds through the following approximations:
    \begin{align*}
        \omega_V
        \xrightarrow{\mathrm{LI}}\omega_{V_{X_R\cup Y_R}}
        \xrightarrow{\text{remove connecting terms}}\omega_{V_{X_R}+V_{Y_R}}
        \xrightarrow{\text{QBP}}\varphi_L.
    \end{align*}
    Each arrow means that the covariance is replaced by the covariance in the state on its right, with an error estimated below. The first step uses LI, the second uses exponential locality of $V$, and the third uses the QBP operators localized in a region $X_{R + L} \cup Y_{R+ L}$. The covariance in $\varphi_L$ is then written as covariances of the initial state $\omega$ and estimated by clustering.

    The parameters $R$ and $L$ balance these errors. Increasing $R$ improves the LI estimate and increasing $L$ improves the QBP localization estimates. On the other hand, increasing them also increases the QBP norms and the supports entering the clustering estimate. We therefore choose them so that the relevant volumes grow sublinearly in $d(X, Y)$. This gives the stretched-exponential bound in the theorem. Decay of correlations that is only polynomial in the distance would not suffice for this argument. For a tracial reference state the final QBP step is unnecessary and a radius proportional to the separation can be used. Thus the loss is a limitation of the estimates and does not rule out exponential clustering of the perturbed state.

    All constants and lower bounds on $d(X,Y)$ used below are independent of $X$, $Y$, $A$ and $B$. They depend only on the lattice, $c$, $\beta$, the interaction norms and the stated clustering and LI data. We specify more restricted dependencies where they are useful. The bound $|\omega_V(AB)-\omega_V(A)\omega_V(B)|\leq2\norm A\norm B$ includes bounded distances after increasing the constant in the theorem, so we may restrict to sufficiently large $d(X,Y)$.

\subsubsection*{Restricting the perturbation to two separated regions}
    
    Fix $\vartheta \coloneq 1 / (D + 2)$. We divide the proof into two cases and first consider the case $1 + |X| + |Y| \leq(1+d (X, Y))^\vartheta$. The complementary case will follow from the elementary covariance bound at the end of the proof.
    We define the fattening parameter
    \[
        R\coloneq \left\lfloor \left(\frac{(1+d(X, Y))^\vartheta}{1 + |X| + |Y|}\right)^{1/D} \right\rfloor.
    \]
    The denominator in this choice compensates for the sizes of the original supports. Since $R\geq1$, the volume bound gives, with $C_0=2^DC_\mathrm{vol}$ depending only on the lattice,
    \begin{align}
        |X_R|+|Y_R| \leq C_0 (1+d (X, Y))^\vartheta. \label{eq: proof generalized doc size U W}
    \end{align}
    Furthermore, $d(X_R,Y_R) \geq d(X,Y)-2R$. Since $R \leq (1+d(X, Y))^{\vartheta/D}$ and $\vartheta/D<1$, it holds that
    \begin{align}
        d(X_R, Y_R)\geq \frac {d(X, Y)}{2}
        \label{eq: proof generalized doc distance U W}
    \end{align}
    for all sufficiently large distances. 

    We next use LI to replace the limiting perturbation by the perturbation supported in $X_R\cup Y_R$. Let $(\Lambda_j)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}$ be an IAS and set $V_j\coloneq V_{\Lambda_j}$. For all sufficiently large $j$, it holds that $X_R \cup Y_R \subseteq\Lambda_j$ and that $d(X\cup Y,\Lambda_j\setminus(X_R\cup Y_R)) \geq R$. LI applied to $AB\in\mathcal{A}_{X\cup Y}$ gives
    \begin{align*}
        |\omega_{V_j}(AB)-\omega_{V_{X_R\cup Y_R}}(AB)|
        \leq
        \lVert A\rVert\lVert B\rVert
        f_\mathrm{LI}(|X\cup Y|)
        g_\mathrm{LI}(\lVert V\rVert_{\exp(-c\cdot)})
        \zeta_\mathrm{LI}(R).
    \end{align*}
    Applying LI also to $A$ and $B$ and using that states have norm one gives
    \begin{align*}
        &|\omega_{V_j}(A)\omega_{V_j}(B)
        -\omega_{V_{X_R\cup Y_R}}(A)\omega_{V_{X_R \cup Y_R}}(B)|\\
        & \quad \leq
        2\lVert A\rVert\lVert B\rVert
        f_\mathrm{LI}(|X| + |Y|)
        g_\mathrm{LI}(\lVert V\rVert_{\exp(-c\cdot)})
        \zeta_\mathrm{LI}(R).
    \end{align*}
    Combining the preceding estimates, taking $j\to\infty$, using Proposition \ref{prop: convergence from li} and the assumptions on $f_\mathrm{LI}$ and $\zeta_\mathrm{LI}$, we obtain
    \begin{align*}
        &\big|
        \omega_V(AB)-\omega_V(A)\omega_V(B)
        -\omega_{V_{X_R\cup Y_R}}(AB)
        +\omega_{V_{X_R\cup Y_R}}(A)
         \omega_{V_{X_R\cup Y_R}}(B)
        \big|
        \\
        & \qquad \leq
        C_1 \lVert A\rVert\lVert B\rVert
            \exp\bigl(C_\mathrm{LI}(1+|X|+|Y|)\bigr) \mathrm{e}^{-\mu_\mathrm{LI} R}, \addtocounter{equation}{1}\tag{\theequation} \label{eq: proof generalized doc LI}
    \end{align*}
    where $C_1=3C_\mathrm{LI}^2g_\mathrm{LI}(\norm V_{\exp(-c\cdot)})$ depends only on the LI data and the interaction norm. In the next step, we remove the terms connecting $X_R$ and $Y_R$.
    \begin{align*}
        V_{X_R\cup Y_R} - V_{X_R} - V_{Y_R} = \sum_{\substack{Z\subseteq (X_R\cup Y_R)\\
        Z\nsubseteq X_R,\;Z\nsubseteq Y_R}}V(Z).
    \end{align*}
    Every set in this sum meets both $X_R$ and $Y_R$, and therefore has diameter at least $d(X_R,Y_R)$. Consequently,
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert V_{X_R\cup Y_R}-V_{X_R}-V_{Y_R} \rVert
        &\leq
        \sum_{x\in X_R}
        \sum_{\substack{x\in Z\in P_0(\Gamma)\\
        \mathrm{diam}(Z)\geq d(X_R, Y_R)}}
        \lVert V(Z)\rVert\\
        &\leq
        |X_R|\mathrm{e}^{-c\,d(X_R,Y_R)}
        \lVert V\rVert_{\exp(-c\cdot)}.
    \end{align*}
    Equations \eqref{eq: proof generalized doc size U W} and \eqref{eq: proof generalized doc distance U W} yield
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert V_{X_R\cup Y_R}-V_{X_R}-V_{Y_R}\rVert
        \leq C_0 \, \lVert V \rVert_{\exp(- c \cdot)} \,  (1 + d (X, Y))^\vartheta\mathrm{e}^{- c d (X, Y)/2},
    \end{align*}
    Applying Corollary \ref{cor: cont. KMS state} to $AB$, $A$ and $B$ gives
    \begin{align}
        &\big|
        \omega_{V_{X_R \cup Y_R}}(AB)
        -\omega_{V_{X_R \cup Y_R}}(A)\omega_{V_{X_R \cup Y_R}}(B)
        -\omega_{V_{X_R} + V_{Y_R}} (AB)
        +\omega_{V_{X_R} + V_{Y_R}}(A) \omega_{V_{X_R} + V_{Y_R}} (B)
        \big|\nonumber\\
        & \quad \leq
        3|\beta|\lVert A\rVert \, \lVert B\rVert
        \lVert V_{X_R \cup Y_R} - V_{X_R} - V_{Y_R} \rVert\nonumber\\
        & \quad \leq 3 |\beta| \, C_0 \, \lVert V \rVert_{\exp(- c \cdot)} \, \lVert A \rVert \, \lVert B \rVert \, (1+d (X, Y))^\vartheta\mathrm{e}^{- c\, d(X, Y)/2}.
        \label{eq: proof generalized doc remove crossing}
    \end{align}


\subsubsection*{Localizing the two QBP operators}

    It remains to estimate the covariance in $\omega_{V_{X_R}+V_{Y_R}}$. We first express this state in terms of $\omega$. Let $\eta_{X_R}$ be the QBP operator which transports $\omega$ to $\omega_{V_{X_R}}$, and let $\eta_{Y_R}^{X_R}$ transport $\omega_{V_{X_R}}$ to $\omega_{V_{X_R}+V_{Y_R}}$. Applying Theorem \ref{thm: QBP} twice gives
    \begin{align*}
        \omega_{V_{X_R}+V_{Y_R}}(D)
        =\omega\bigl(\eta_{X_R}\eta_{Y_R}^{X_R}D(\eta_{Y_R}^{X_R})^*\eta_{X_R}^*\bigr),
        \qquad D\in\mathcal{A}_\Gamma.
    \end{align*}
    The second operator depends on the first perturbation, but its generator is localized near $Y_R$. We will approximate the two operators separately, by localizing their generators before taking the Dyson series. This also gives control of the norms of the local approximations.

    We first choose the decay exponents so that Proposition \ref{prop: loc of phi} applies at the fixed inverse temperature $\beta$. Let $\kappa_{c,c/2}$ be the constant from Proposition \ref{prop: LiebRobinson}, which depends only on the lattice and $c$. By increasing this constant if necessary, we assume that $\kappa_{c,c/2}\geq1$. We choose $b=c/2$ and
    \begin{align*}
        0<a&<\min \bigl(
        \frac c{16},
        \frac{\pi c}{64\kappa_{c,c/2}(1+|\beta|)
        \bigl(1+\norm H_{\exp(-c\cdot)}+2\norm V_{\exp(-c\cdot)}\bigr)}
        \bigr).
    \end{align*}
